he House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
FAREWELL TO THE RETIRING MEMBER 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Honourable Members, today, we bid farewell to our esteemed 
colleague, Shri Ranjan Gogai , nominated Member of this house, who is retiring 
today, that is, 16th of March, 2026, on completion of his term of office.  
S
hri Ranjan Gogai, former Chief Justice of India, began his association with the 
august in March, 2020, following his nomination by the honourable President of India. 
As a distinguished jurist, he brought unparalleled legal acumen and experience to the 
deliberations of the Rajya Sabha. His intervention in the Rajya Sabha reflected his 
deep understanding of legislative processes and public interest. The House will, 
certainly, miss his wise counsel, measured intervention and the gravitas he brought to 
our discussions. I wish that he will continue to contribute to the nation in different 
capacities with the same dedication, integrity and the spirit of service that he has 
exemplified throughout his illustrious career.  
On behalf of the entire House, I extend my best wishes to Shri Ranjan Gogai 
for good health, happiness and many more years of fruitful service to the nation.  
MR. CHAIRMAN: Papers to be laid on the Table.

ANNOUNCEMENT BY THE CHAIR 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Members, during the meeting of the Business Advisory 
Committee held on 11 th March, 2026, certain honourable Members had suggested 
th
at, in view of the commencement of several auspicious festivals, such as Navratri, 
Gudi Parva, Ramadan, etc., from 19 th March onwards, it would be appropriate if the 
s
ittings of the Council scheduled for 19th and 20th March, 2026, were not held.   
I
 wish to inform the honourable Members that the request made in this regard 
has been considered favourably by the Government. Accordingly, the sittings of the 
Council scheduled for Thursday, the 19 th March, 2026, and Friday, the 20 th March, 
2
026, stands cancelled.  
However, in order to make up for the time so lost, the Council will instead sit 
on Saturday, the 28th March, 2026, and Sunday, the 29th March, 2026.  
Ho
nourable Members may kindly take note of the same.                                                         
SHRI DEREK O’BRIEN (West Bengal): Sir, I wish to make a point. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: What is your point? 
SHRI DEREK O’BRIEN : Sir, I do not at all want to disturb the Zero Hour nor have 
given notice under Rule 267.

 Now, Shrimati Sudha Murty.  
Demand to establish an AIIMS at Raichur in Karnataka 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated): I am standing here today on behalf of the 
peo
ple of Karnataka, especially on behalf of the people of Kalyana Karnataka (North 
Karnataka), to make a request to the Central Government. Our Central Government 
has established All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS) in many states, but 
there is none in Karnataka. For this reason, I request the honourable Government to 
please consider our Karnataka. I am not asking for this hospital for Hubballi or 
Bengaluru; I am asking for Raichur district. Raichur district is very backward. 
According to NITI Aayog, it is one of the many backward regions. By the grace of the 
kings of the Kingdom of Mysore, South Karnataka has grown to be prosperous. But, 
in the last 250 years, under the rule of others, North Karnataka has been neglected or 
forgotten. There are no proper healthcare facilities there. There is extreme poverty, 
health issues and a lack of education. Even if there are institutions, they are not of 
high quality. It is because of this, the poor people there spend more on their 
healthcare than those living in Bengaluru or other large areas. If they have any health 
problems, they have to travel to distant Bengaluru or Hyderabad. In such a situation, 
it would be very beneficial if an AIIMS is established there. 
Sir,  as per Article 371(J) of the Constitution of India, if such institutions are 
established there, 70 per cent of the seats will go to the local people, and by 
obtaining degrees there, they can serve the people. If you look at the statistics of 
Karnataka, the lack of education is very high in Raichur district. Only 48% of women 
are educated, and similarly, only 60% of men are educated. Due to the lack of large 
hospitals here, people suffer greatly from health problems. Recalling a kirtana 
mentioned by Gopaladas, I request our honorable Government and our honourable

one person, and it is a small district, this is our request because it will be of great help 
to the poor people.

Demand for preservation of old Koirengei Airfield of Manipur as part of  
India's national heritage 
SHRI MAHARAJA SANAJAOBA LEISHEMBA (Manipur):  Thank you, Sir. My Zero 
Hour mention is -‘Demand for preservation of old Koirengei airfield of Manipur as part 
of India's national heritage’. Sir, I draw the kind attention of the Government 
regarding the historical significance of the old Koirengei airfield of Manipur and urgent 
need for its preservation as part of India's national heritage. It was constructed during 
1942-43 with the assistance of American engineers. This airfield played a crucial role 
in the Allied Defence during the Battle of Imphal in World War-II. It served as a key 
supply and support base for the Allied Forces and played an important role to stop 
Japanese advance into India.  
Earlier, in 1942, when Japanese forces advanced into Burma, the Imphal valley 
became a major route for the evacuation of civilians. This airfield served as an 
important humanitarian corridor where displaced families, children and the sick 
received food, shelter and medical assistance. This airfield supported the vital air-
bridge that sustained nearly 2,00,000 Allied troops and helped secure India’s Eastern 
frontier. It was the last departure point of Major General Orde Wingate and later 
served as the landing ground where India’s first Prime Minister, Nehru ji, was formally 
welcomed in Manipur.                                                   
 However, the physical remains of this airfield are gradually disappearing due to 
unregulated constructions and military presence. People of Manipur are also very 
disappointed to see the deteriorating condition of the airfield. Preserving this site will 
protect its historical legacy and will enable future generations to understand India’s 
wartime resilience and humanitarian commitment. I, therefore, urge the Government 
to take up necessary steps, in coordination with the Ministry concerned and the State 
Government, to preserve and protect the historical ‘Old Koirengei Airfield’ as a 
National Heritage Airfield Site. Thank you, Sir.                  
[ 16 March, 2026 ]

 Dr. K. Laxman.  
Demand for inclusion of State OBC castes in the Central OBC list 
DR. K. LAXMAN (Uttar Pradesh): Thank you, Chairman, Sir, for giving this 
opportunity to raise an important issue pertaining to OBCs in various States. I rise to 
draw the attention of the House to a longstanding demand for the inclusion of several 
OBC castes from Telangana, Andhra Pradesh, Rajasthan, Gujarat, and Maharashtra 
into the Central OBC list.   
Sir, the National Commission for Backward Classes has already recommended 
the Union Government for inclusion of 27 castes from Telangana and five castes from 
Andhra Pradesh in the Central OBC List. However, the matter of grave concern is that 
these 27 and five castes from both the States are though in the State Lists as OBCs, 
yet despite their social, educational, and economic backwardness, they are not being 
treated as OBCs under the Union Government for central educational institutes and 
Union Government job opportunities. At the State level, they are getting their due 
share as OBCs according to their respective percentages in their respective States.  
But, when it comes to availing benefits of job reservations in the Central universities, 
railways, communications, and even to procure a seat in IIT, where quality education 
is being provided by the Modiji Government, they are not able to avail that benefit. For 
the first time, after Modi ji came to power, 27 per cent reservation has been given in 
educational institutions to all OBCs, whether it is Kendriya Vidyalaya, Navodaya 
Vidyalaya, Sainik schools, or law universities.  Even in NEET, for the first time the 
MBBS seats have also been increased to the maximum extent. But unfortunately, 
these people, who hail from the backward caste communities from these States, are 
not being eligible. So, I urge upon the Government to address this long-pending 
demand at the earliest, so that the recommendations made by the NCBC may be 
implemented to extend the benefits of reservation in public appointments, 
employment, and public educational institutions, under the purview of the Union 
Government, and thereby ensuring social justice, equality of opportunity, and the 
promotion of inclusive development. Sir, I am confident that the Government led 
by Modiji will consider the matter sympathetically and on humanitarian grounds, and 
12
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
would take all necessary steps to fulfill the mandate of the Constitution and would 
res
pond to the aspirations of this deserving community. Thank you, Sir.

 Shriman Haris Beeran . 
Demand for legislation on withdrawal and withholding of life-sustaining  
medical treatment for terminally-ill patients 
SHRI HARISH BEERAN (Kerala):  Thank you, Chairman, Sir, for giving me this 
opportunity. Sir, I want to draw the attention of the House to a very serious matter.  
Last week, the Supreme Court had delivered a judgement by which it allowed the 
withdrawal of life support system to a terminally-ill patient. Shri Harish Rana, a healthy 
young man, who had an accidental fall in 2013 from the fourth floor of his paying guest 
accommodation, was in coma for the entire 13 years. He was in permanent vegetative 
state, 100 per cent disabled and was on clinically assisted nutrition and hydration. His 
parents did not have any other option but to move the Court. I spoke to their lawyer, 
Rashmi Nandakumar, a friend of mine, who told me that the resilience shown by the 
family during this entire personal trauma was commendable.  
 Sir, while delivering the judgement, the Supreme Court Judges, Justice 
Jamshed Pardiwala and Justice K. V.  Viswanathan, with tears in their eyes, told the 
parents that  “You are not giving  up on your son,  you are allowing him to leave with 
dignity.” The Supreme Court also realised that they are stepping into an arena where 
there is no legislation.  Article 21 of the Constitution says that there is a Right to Life 
14
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
with dignity. That has been stretched to Right to Die with dignity. But, how much 
mor
e can the Supreme Court stretch is the question? My issue, Sir, is that there is no 
legislation despite all these things. In 2006, 196 th Law Commission Report 
com
prehensively examined the issue of passive euthanasia, appended a draft law, 
but Parliament did nothing. In 2011, the Supreme Court was forced to step in Aruna 
Shanbaug’s case and framed guidelines because the Parliament did nothing. In 2012, 
241st Report of the Law Commission, again, had a draft law, but the Parliament did 
noth
ing.  In 2018, the Constitution Bench of the Supreme Court in Common Cause 
case issued elaborate guidelines noting that they will operate only till the Parliament 
framed a law, but the Parliament did nothing. In 2023, the Supreme Court modified its 
guidelines till the time the Parliament enacts a law, the Parliament did nothing.   In 
2026, the latest judgement of the Supreme Court also expresses the hope that 
Parliament legislates on the subject. 
 Sir, we talk endlessly about protecting the poor from financial distress. Over 65 
per cent of all healthcare expenditure in our country is paid entirely out-of-pocket by 
the families. Yet, we refuse to pass a law. Sir, no doctor or a healthcare professional 
will be prepared to take the risk. Also, Sir, there are similar legislations in the foreign 
countries, but we are not prepared.    
 Sir, my State Kerala has shown the nation what dignified end of life looks like.  
Since 2008, Kerala has been running a community-based palliative care programme,   
reaching every Gram Panchayat, integrating 500 NGOs. Sir, I urge the Government to 
introduce a medical treatment of terminally-ill patients (end of life) bill as 
recommended by the Law Commission and to mandate  palliative care infrastructure 
at every district hospital and  Primary Healthcare Centres. Honourable Nadda ji is 
there.  I commend to him, Sir.  Thank you.

Demand for stringent laws to prevent disruptions during international  
events in India 
SHRI JAGGESH (Karnataka): Sir, I wish to raise a matter concerning India's global 
reputation as a reliable host for international events. Firstly, I congratulate the 
Government of India, under the leadership of Shri Narendra Modi, for successfully 
organizing the AI Impact Summit, 2026 in Delhi. The event brought together global 
18
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
leaders, inventors and experts in artificial intelligence and highlighted India's growing 
strength in technology. Innovation enhanced India's global image and increased 
opportunities for investment, partnerships and knowledge sharing. However, I 
strongly condemn the disruptive and irresponsible action by members of Congress 
Party during the Summit. Attempting to disturb such an important international event 
is completely unacceptable and reflects poorly on the country. Such action risks 
public safety and can damage India's reputation before the global community. 
Sir, the Modi Government is working hard to manage the impact of global 
conflicts and ensure stable LPG supplies. But it is unbecoming of a grand old party 
like the Congress Party to spread panic about LPG supply for political advantage 
during a national challenge. It takes many years to build a nation's image, but a single 
careless act can harm it. The nation and its reputation must always come before any 
party or individual. In international platforms, it is India that is represented before the 
world and, therefore, everyone must act with responsibility and keep the national 
interest of our political benefits. I urge the Government to treat this matter with utmost 
seriousness and enact clear and stringent laws to deal with any disruptive actions by 
individuals, groups or political parties during international events held in India. Such 
measures are essential to safeguard public order, protect India's global reputation 
and ensure that such incidents never repeat. Thank you, Sir.   
MR. CHAIRMAN: The following honourable Members associated themselves with the 
matter raised by honourable Member, Shri Jaggesh : Shri Ryaga Krishnaiah (Andhra 
Pradesh), Shri Gulam Ali (Nominated), Shri Maharaja Sanajaoba Leishemba 
(Manipur), Shri Sat Paul Sharma (Jammu and Kashmir), Shrimati Rekha Sharma 
(Haryana) and Dr. Meenakshi Jain (Nominated). 
 Shriman Mallikarjun Kharge.

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
*241. [The questioner was absent.] 
Pilot fatigue and FDTL rules 
*241.    SHRI SAKET GOKHALE: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to 
state: 
(a)  the reasons for granting temporary exemptions to certain airlines from the 
new Flight Duty Time Limitations (FDTL) rules until February 2026; 
(b)  whether the Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA) has conducted a 
safety risk assessment before allowing these exemptions; and 
(c)  the number of fatigue-related safety occurrences reported by pilots during 
this exemption period till date? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU):  (a) to 
(c) A statement is laid on the table of house. 
Statement 
 (a) The 'temporary exemptions' were granted to various airlines from the new 
Flight Duty Time Limitation (FDTL) regulations under CAR Section 7 Series J Part III 
based on operational constraints as submitted by the airlines, subject to review after a 
period of six months from the date of implementation, i.e., 01.11.2025. 
 Further, IndiGo was granted a one-time temporary exemption from two 
clauses of the FDTL regulations, valid from 05.12.2025 to 10.02.2026, in order to 
stabilize the disruptions experienced in December 2025 and to reduce passenger 
inconvenience. 
 (b) Safety Risk Assessment (SRA) is submitted by the airlines and is duly 
examined by DGCA before granting any variation. 
 (c) No fatigue-related safety occurrences have been reported by pilots during 
this exemption period till date. 
30
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Th e questioner is not present. Now, Third supplementary. Dr. John 
Brittas.     
DR. JOHN BRITTAS : Sir, during the Indigo crisis, one aspect was debated and 
discussed - the existence of an airline cartel in this country. Even the honourable 
Minister, while replying to a question, underscored the fact that we need to have 
more airlines to ensure that there is no cartelisation of airlines. One fallout of this 
cartelization has been… 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Please be specific about your question. 
DR. JOHN BRITTAS: Sir, I am coming to the point.  You will also be interested in it.  
An airport like Kannur is denied ‘Point of Call’ facility essentially because of the 
insistence of domestic airlines. In the context of the concerns that have been 
expressed, will the Minister ensure that ‘Point of Call’ facility would be extended to 
Kannur? Thank you, Sir. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Honourable Minister.   
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU:  Sir, this ‘Point of Call’ issue for Kannur has 
been raised multiple times by the honourable Member, Dr. John Brittas, himself, and, 
many a time, when Kerala was discussing civil aviation matters with the Ministry, this 
has been one priority demand from their side. I would also like to apprise the House of 
the ‘Point of Call’ issue. ‘Point of Call’ issue is not related to one State or one airport.  
There are multiple States asking for ‘Point of Call’. ‘Point of Call’ is an airport being 
listed as a destination in the bilateral air service agreements, which we have 
individually, with different countries. Three ‘Point of Call’ facilities are already there in 
Kerala which is the highest among the States. Definitely, we are actively looking at it.  
As far as bilateral air service agreements are concerned, whenever saturation is 
achieved, then, we definitely talk with the concerned States and we look into the 
expansion.  At this ‘Point of Call’ also, we will, definitely, have a look into it.  
MR. CHAIRMAN: Thank you. Fourth supplementary. Dr. Fauzia Khan.  
DR. FAUZIA KHAN:  Sir, will the Minister inform the House how many ordinary 
passengers, including senior citizens and medical travelers, were stranded due to 
over 175 cancellations on a single day at Bengaluru, Delhi and Hyderabad airports?  
What compensation was paid to them and under which legal provision? Does the 
Government accept that crew buffers at Indigo stood at zero per cent in December 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
31

 
2025 and rose to only three per cent by February 2026, which is well below any safe 
operational threshold? If the DGCA was monitoring airlines quarterly, how did this 
dangerous crew shortage go undetected until a full-blown crisis? 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Sir, as far as the requested data of the three 
airports is concerned, I will have to collect that to give it to the honourable Member. I 
do not have that for those airports and for those classes of passengers that has been 
requested. In terms of the compensation that has been paid during that time, the total 
number of impacted customers was supposed to be 3,64,000, and the total 
compensation that was distributed to customers after validation -- there are many 
more passengers who need to validate according to their travel tickets and all – was 
Rs.43.81 crore. It has already been disbursed. The Ministry was in continuous 
engagement with the airlines on the kind of damage or the impact that it has created 
to the passengers. Much more compensation has to be given to the passengers. A 
gesture of care was also extended where travel coupons worth Rs.10,000 were 
extended. For this, Rs.163.92 crore has already been distributed to the passengers.  
And if passengers can validate their time of travel during that specific time, they will 
also be extended that. This is something which the Ministry is continuously following 
up with them. Regarding the incident that has happened, I have also mentioned it in 
the House before that the DGCA has been in continuous engagement with the 
airlines. Whenever there is a new schedule that has been approved, we have multiple 
sittings with it. This was a specific instance where there were new FDTL norms that 
had come into place.  It was for the airlines to look into their internal operations. And 
the airline can say if FDTL is coming into place, how many more pilots we need, how 
we do the re-rostering of the crew so that the impact is not there on the operations. 
We were consulting and discussing with them on a day-to-day basis before the 
implementation. Even after it is done, we are taking much more care right now so that 
this kind of situation doesn't repeat itself. I also have to tell the House that once this 
incident happened, we went and looked into the internal operations; we have also 
suggested for employment of more pilots. More than 246 pilots have been employed 
by IndiGo itself in these three months. So, this is an indication. We have also brought 
down 10 per cent operations of Indigo. So, multiple necessary measures have been 
taken so that this kind of incident doesn't repeat itself.  
MR. CHAIRMAN: Now, fifth supplementary; Prof. Manoj Kumar Jha. Please raise only 
one supplementary, not that you raise five supplementaries in one supplementary.  
32
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
PROF. MANOJ KUMAR JHA : Yes, Sir. I would be very specific. Just 30 second! 
Honourable Chairman, Sir, recently, we have seen certain accidents. The whole 
nation was in mourning for very long. There was that flight from Ahmedabad to 
London; subsequently, an incident involving Ajit Pawarji's death. There are reports 
that the Pilots’ Association wants the entire issue to be investigated under a particular 
schedule because of the mystery surrounding all the cases, including chopper 
crashes. Is the Ministry of Civil Aviation planning to have a much more robust system 
which does not promote opacity? Thank you so much. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Sir, the honourable Member has raised a 
very important question which has also been widely discussed by the public in general 
over the last one year. I have to place the record on the floor of the House that all the 
investigations happen through Aircraft Accident Investigation Bureau which has all the 
resources to do proper investigation in a thorough, transparent and fair manner. All 
the investigations are done through standards and protocols set up by the 
International Civil Aviation Organization. It is not that India is just deciding how I will do 
the investigation. These are international civil aviation standards where we are all part 
of those treaties and we all follow those international guidelines and standards. 
Accident can happen anywhere in the world. Their investigation has to follow certain 
standards and procedures. That is the first thing.  The second thing is about AAIB. 
From the Government, we have been continuously trying to strengthen the 
organization. Most of the times, what happens is, in parts of investigation, there are 
many of the investigated things. For example, if I take the Black Box, all these were 
supposed to be sent to the OEM itself where the part was getting manufactured. To 
decode it, all these products were sent outside. But, for the first time, we have had 
the decoding lab set up in AAIB itself. This was two years ago. Now, all the major 
data decoding from FDRs, etc., is happening in the country itself. That is another 
thing that we have the full authority on the data that is there. The third thing that I 
would also like to mention is this. In respect of AAIB and the accidents that are 
happening, we are slowly improving the resources that are required so that there is no 
dependency on any other organization. All the investigations are thoroughly done by 
the AAIB itself and in future also, we are trying to give more strength to the 
organization that whenever these kinds of incidents happen, all the investigation 
happens in the country itself. Thank you.

(a) the details of households provided access to potable tap water 
connections since 2022 under Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) and the 
percentage it constitutes of rural population; 
(b) whether the JMM in the past has been marred with irregularities; 
(c) if so, the details thereof; and 
(d) the steps taken by Government for ground inspection of the JMM works? 
THE MINISTER OF JAL SHAKTI (SHRI C R PATIL): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on 
the table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) Since August 2019, Government of India is implementing Jal Jeevan 
Mission (JJM) – Har Ghar Jal, in partnership with States/ UTs, to make provision of 
safe and adequate tap water connection to every rural household of the country. As 
reported by States/UTs on JJM IMIS, more than 8.53 crore rural households have 
been provided with tap water connections since financial year 2021-22, which 
constitutes about 44% of the total rural households in the country. Thus, as on date 
the overall coverage of rural households with tap water connections has increased 
from 3.23 crore (16.72%) in 2019 to 15.82 crore (81.74%). 
  (b) and (c) As per the data reported by all States/ UTs, a total of 18,790 
complaints have been received by States/ UTs from different sources such as media 
reports, suo-moto cognizance, references from public representatives, citizens, 
grievance portal, etc. regarding financial irregularities and poor quality of works under 
JJM. States/ UTs have also reported that action has been taken against 635 
Departmental Officials, 1,020 contractors, and 155 Third Party Inspection Agencies 
(TPIAs). The State-wise details of complaints received and action taken against 
officials, contractors and third-party agencies are annexed.  
  Furthermore, States/ UTs have been repeatedly advised to adopt a zero-
tolerance approach toward any financial, procedural, or quality-related violations. All 
States/ UTs have been advised to ensure that every complaint is duly examined, field 
verification is carried out promptly and all required disciplinary, contractual, and legal 
34
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
actions are taken without exception to uphold transparency and accountability of the 
miss
ion. 
  (d)  To ensure the successful implementation of JJM, Government of India 
has been regularly reviewing the implementation with respective State Governments 
through review meetings and visits of multi-disciplinary teams to highlight the areas to 
strengthen implementation and monitoring of the mission. Further, several initiatives 
have been undertaken to strengthen the monitoring mechanism under JJM through 
ground inspection of water supply schemes, are as under:  
(i) States have been conducting monthly inspections of four schemes each, 
assigned by the Department of Drinking Water and Sanitation (DDWS), to 
strengthen field-level oversight. State-level teams inspect the schemes to 
assess the quality of construction, adherence to timelines, availability of 
services, and resolution of issues and submit their reports on JJM –IMIS. 
(ii) Central Nodal Officers were appointed by Government of India for ground 
truthing of JJM schemes and the reports submitted by them have been shared 
with States/ UTs for necessary corrective measures.  
(iii) Ground verification has been enhanced by strengthening the monitoring 
framework for National WASH Experts (NWEs) through a revised and 
comprehensive checklist emphasizing quality of execution, along with a revised 
Terms of Reference (ToR) for TPIs to ensure effective supervision.  
(iv) Central Prabhari Officers (CPOs) for Aspirational Blocks and Aspirational 
districts have also been sensitized to review rural piped water supply schemes 
under JJM during their field visits and have been provided with a digital 
reporting mechanism. 
(v) Under Jal Seva Adhyan, a recent initiative of Department of Drinking Water and 
Sanitation, in collaboration with All India Council of Technical Evaluation, 
engineering students under the guidance of technical mentors have undertaken 
assessment of piped water supply schemes and share their observations with 
the Department. 
(vi) Jal Seva Aankalan is a digital tool enabling community-led self-review process 
and ground verification process, through which villages assess the 
performance of their piped water supply systems on parameters such as 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
35

 
regularity, adequacy, quality and system maintenance, thus fostering Jan 
Bhagidari. 
(vii) IT monitoring architecture has also been expanded. Apart from State-level 
access, District Water and Sanitation Mission (DWSM) officials and Gram 
Panchayat-level functionaries are being onboarded onto the IMIS, enabling 
decentralized monitoring and improved grassroots-level oversight.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Q. No. 243.   
Installation of advanced weather forecasting and air traffic management systems 
*243.    SHRIMATI SULATA DEO : Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to 
state: 
42
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
(a)  whether Government proposes to install advanced weather forecasting and 
air 
traffic management systems at airports in cyclone-prone coastal area in 
the State of Odisha; and 
(b)  if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU):  (a) 
and (b) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) and (b) Continuous efforts are being taken by the Government of India to 
improve the meteorological infrastructure in Odisha, including the Installation of 
advanced weather observations, monitoring and forecasting system at airports in 
cyclone - prone coastal Odisha. Currently, Odisha has a total of five operational 
airports. All these airports have been supported with advance current weather 
monitoring Instruments. Further, the airport at Bhubaneswar is supported with latest 
Integrated Aviation weather automatic stations and Runway Visibility Reporting 
(RVRs) systems for helping flights to land during bad weather owing to Cyclone, 
heavy rains, low clouds, poor visibility etc. The State has also been equipped with 
state of art Doppler Weather Radar (DWRs) networks with 2 DWR installed at Paradip 
and Gopalpur especially to help in monitoring and tracking Cyclones and provide 
advance warning over the airports. 
 A Cyclone Warning Centre (CWC) is functional in IMD, Bhubaneswar to 
support all aviation related operations for the State during the cyclone period. All 
aviation related bulletins and warnings including cyclone warning, Aerodrome 
Forecasts and airfield warnings (local) and area forecast are provided by IMD, 
Bhubaneswar. 
 Further, a new Air Traffic Management (ATM) Automation System has been 
commissioned at Bhubaneswar Airport on 06.02.2024 by the Airports Authority of 
India. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: First supplementary – Shrimati Sulata Deo.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister.  
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Sir, the honourable Member has asked about 
the impact of cyclones on civil aviation operations, especially in coastal areas. I truly 
understand the seriousness with which she has put the question because I also come 
from Srikakulam, which is right adjacent to Odisha and is also a coastal area. The 
cyclones that have been mentioned, be it 1999 cyclone or Hudhud or Phailin, all these 
cyclones affected Srikakulam in a big way. But, when it comes to civil aviation 
operations, the weather data or the meteorological data is provided by the IMD, 
which is a different department. Taking the data that has been provided by them, 
aircraft operations are carried out in a very safe and secure manner. Regarding the 
infrastructure that has been set up in Odisha for getting better weather-related 
information, Integrated Aviation Weather Automatic Stations and Runway Visibility 
Reporting Systems have been set up at Bhubaneswar airport, which is the 
international and biggest airport in Odisha. The State has also been equipped with 
state-of-the-art Doppler Weather Radars. Two DWRs have been installed at 
Paradeep and Gopalpur. These are both coastal locations and they provide us with 
data on how the cyclones are coming; the tracking and monitoring of cyclones 
happens through these DWRs. And, two DWRs have already been mentioned. A 
Cyclone Warning Centre (CWC) is also functional in IMD Bhubaneswar, which also 
supports weather-related data. All this data is compiled by IMD and provided to the 
airports, the navigation systems, the aircraft and the pilots, so that their operations 
are not hampered by weather. Thank you. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Second supplementary, Srimati Sulata Deo.  
SHRIMATI SULATA DEO : Mr. Chairman, Sir. My second supplementary is whether 
the Government has allocated any specific Budget or financial assistance under the 
aviation infrastructure modernisation scheme for installing advanced meteorological 
equipment, satellite-based navigation systems and upgrading air traffic control 
technologies at airports located in the coastal and cyclone-prone regions of Odisha. 
Thank you. 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon ourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU:  Sir, the upgradation of Air Traffic Control 
machines or the systems, the avionics, everything is done in a very regular and 
periodic manner. Not only it relates to weather-related or IMD-related systems, under 
AI, we have the ATC and navigation systems and they are placed at each and every 
ATC tower that is there in our airports. All of these undergo a regular upgradation 
process depending upon the technological advancements that happen in that sector 
and the world- class standards that we observe. We want our civil aviation in India 
also to be of a global standard. So, this continuous process is there. Infrastructure 
development happens continuously. Then, ATM Automation System is also integrated 
with our weather related systems. Also, different levels of ATCOs, the ever-increasing 
air traffic of Indian FIR, all these things are taken into account when we upgrade our 
systems. Then, sufficient infrastructure spending is also done, so that all the systems 
are not outdated and everything is functional and connected.

SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Sir, regarding the investment in 
manufacturing in aviation, we have taken it up very, very seriously. For the first time, I 
am very happy to tell the House also, the Embraer, which is a Brazilian aircraft 
manufacturing company, has come into a joint venture with the Indian manufacturing 
company, and we are going to very soon start a Final Assembly Line in the country, 
which is going to improve the manufacturing ecosystem. This is not a very easy sector 
to start with, Sir. Aircraft manufacturing is very complex and very precise engineering 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
45

 
takes place. There are countries which have had this project, of building their own 
a
ircraft, for many decades and some have not been successful also. That is why, 
when you look at the international level, only handfuls of countries are able to 
manufacture the aircraft in today's age. But today, under the visionary leadership of 
our honourable Prime Minister, we are going with the attitude that everything is 
possible in this new India that is going through.  So, in this set up, we have strongly 
associated ourselves. We have collaborated with international companies. One I have 
mentioned, which is Embraer.  Also, the Russian firm, Sukhoi and other people, has 
also entered into a MoU with HAL to manufacture the SJ-100 Superjet, which is a 
100-seater and very, very important for our regional connectivity also. On top of that, 
our Regional Transport Aircraft Programme is also being given a lot of strength and 
boost right now. Sir, we have a lot of aircraft component manufacturing that is 
happening today, because we are being seen as a very trusted and loyal 
manufacturing partner and a supply chain manager for the whole world itself. So, with 
that status, more and more manufacturing facilities are being set up in the country 
itself, especially for aviation and aerospace ecosystem. And, in the coming days, as 
we improve the aircraft components and also the Final Assembly Line, we are going 
to see more investments coming in the manufacturing sector also.

Indian carriers have cancelled up to 4,335 flights and foreign carriers have cancelled 
up to 1,187 flights. The first thing we have to remember during this crisis is that the 
safety of the operations is the topmost priority. When the airspace itself is closed, 
there is no reason for us to operate in this area. But with constant communication 
with the air regulators in those specific countries, we have had 24x7 communications 
46
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
with them and whenever there was a slot available for us to operate, we have 
ope

crisis time also and we have ensured safe operations because our DGCA, our Aviation 
Department, everyone is in thorough collaboration with the Authorities there. Right 
now, there is some airspace which is still closed and we are trying to do our best to 
facilitate for them. But, first and foremost, the airspace has to be open and it has to 
be safe to travel. Only then we can have regular travel taking place.

Scheme to expand the airport 
*2
44.    SHRI KHIRU MAHTO: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to state: 
(a)  whether Government is formulating a scheme to expand the Ranchi-
Bokaro airport; 
(b)  if so, the details thereof; 
(c)  whether Government has set a deadline in this regard; and 
(d)  if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU):  (a) to 
(d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) to (d) The expansion and upgradation of airports is a continuous process 
and is undertaken by the Airport Operators from time to time, depending on the 
availability of land, commercial viability, socio-economic considerations, traffic 
demand/willingness of airlines to operate to/from such airports. The Airports 
Authority of India (AAI) has undertaken construction of a Parallel Taxi Track (PTT), 
Rapid Exit Track (RET) and reconfiguration of the existing Terminal Building to 
augment the capacity of Ranchi Airport. The timeline for completion of the airport 
projects depends upon many factors, such as land acquisition, availability of 
mandatory clearances, financial closure, etc. 
 Bokaro Airport, owned by Steel Authority of India Limited (SAIL), has been 
identified for development under the Regional Connectivity Scheme - 
UdeDeshkaAamNagrik (RCS-UDAN). An amount of Rs. 78.50 crore has been 
incurred towards the development of the airport infrastructure. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Third supplementary; Shri Satnam Singh Sandhu.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Honourable Chairman, Sir, another Member 
had also asked specifically about the point of call. I have said that this point of call 
issue is not for just one specific airport. There are multiple airports and multiple States 
have been requesting points of call for better international connectivity. If you look at 
Chandigarh, they have connectivity to Abu Dhabi and Dubai also right now. The point 
of call is a requirement when foreign carriers have to come to our specific airports. If 
our domestic airlines, Indian carriers, have to travel anywhere across the world, 
based on the BASA, Bilateral Air Service Agreement, they can travel from any of the 
airports. It has to be an international airport, and Chandigarh is an international 
airport. As for international air connectivity, I wish to make a couple of points, so that 
people understand the whole argument around these Air Service Agreements. No.1, 
definitely, we want the first advantage to be taken by our Indian carriers. It is our 
responsibility as Government too to encourage more of our Indian carriers to take 
international traffic in our own aircraft. For that, we are trying to see how best we can 
facilitate our Indian carriers. Secondly, we want our hubs to also grow. We have 
metropolitan cities like Delhi, Mumbai, Bangalore, but what happens is, when you 
give more points of call, the foreign carriers come and all the international passengers 
are taken to their country. They offer the transit airport and, from there, they try to 
send them to other destinations. Why can we not do it? Why can’t Delhi, Mumbai, 
Bangalore, Hyderabad or Kolkata become international hubs? It is with that idea also 
we want to encourage our Indian carriers to take more and more people. So, while 
keeping this intact, we have given many points of call across the country. And 
definitely, if there is a very specific requirement, like honourable Member has 
mentioned about the Chandigarh Airport and the traffic that it is generating over there, 
the Government would, definitely, look into it.

years has never been observed before. Almost 40 per cent traffic has improved in 
Tamil Nadu. In Chennai itself, Airports Authority of India is taking up a new terminal 
building which is costing up to Rs. 2,500 crores and we are going to inaugurate it in a 
few months itself. Salem Airport has been operationalised under RCS-UDAN Scheme 
and now good connectivity is being provided there. Tuticorin Airport is now renamed 
50
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
as ‘Thoothukudi’ because in Tamil it was written as Thoothukudi and in English and 
Hindi, it was written as Tuticorin. To decolonise the whole naming and to restore the

declared as international airport. Coimbatore is the second largest city in Tamil Nadu

principle clearance. But, for that, the State has to send a proposal.

*245. [The questioner was absent.] 
Surveys conducted under JJM using Google images 
*245. DR. KANIMOZHI NVN SOMU: Will the Minister of JAL SHAKTI be pleased to 
state: 
(a)  Whether Government is aware that surveys under the Jal Jeevan Mission 
(JJM) have been conducted using Google images instead of door-to-door 
survey and if so, the details thereof; 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
51

 
(b)  Whether these surveys have resulted in a large number of households 
b
eing left out, incorrect data being reported and water connections being 
provided without having concern for water supply; 
(c)  If so, the details thereof; and 
(d)  The corrective action taken by Government in this regard? 
THE MINISTER OF JAL SHAKTI (SHRI C R PATIL): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on 
the table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) to (d) The Government of India is driving a comprehensive transformation 
of the nation’s water landscape through its flagship initiative, the Jal Jeevan Mission 
(JJM) – Har Ghar Jal. Since August 2019, Government of India, in partnership with 
States, is implementing Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM)-Har Ghar Jal to enable every rural 
household in the country, to have assured potable water through tap connection.  
 Water being a State subject, the responsibility of planning, approval, 
implementation, operation, and maintenance of drinking water supply schemes/ 
works, including those under the Jal Jeevan Mission, lies with State/UT 
Governments. The Government of India supports the States by providing technical 
and financial assistance only. If any cases of Google images being used for survey or 
any such deviations from procedure are reported, necessary action is taken by States 
as per extant rules. 
 Further, to enhance transparency and to ensure technically sound 
implementation of Jal Jeevan Mission and also to ensure ground truthing of assets, 
the Sujalam Bharat database is a digital platform designed for  States/UTs to 
systematically map, geotag, and monitor rural water supply assets. It enablesfield-
level data capture and validation for  bulk water , out-village, and  in-
village components, generating unique  Sujal Gaon IDs . As a foundational Digital 
Public Infrastructure (DPI),  it creates a GIS-based digital asset registry, linking 
infrastructure components to ensure a seamless, transparent, and data-driven supply 
chain from source to household. 
 As per the Operational Guidelines of ‘Jal Jeevan Mission’, it is the responsibility 
of National Jal Jeevan Mission (NJJM) to carry out regular functionality assessment, 
evaluation and impact assessment. For the purpose, NJJM selects a 3rd party 
agency through open tendering. In such assessment(s), State/ UTs are given a 
52
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
functionality score on the basis of functionality of samples on quantity (55lpcd or 
mor
e), quality and regularity i.e. water supply to rural households for all 12 months in 
a year or on daily basis. All these parameters are taken to define the functionality of 
the HH tap water connections, as per specified criteria. 
 Since starting of the mission in August 2019, three functionality assessment 
survey has been conducted in 2020- 21, 2021-22 and 2024-25. The reports of the last 
functionality assessment 2024 is also available in public domain at following URL: 
https://jaljeevanmission.gov.in/functionality-report-2024 
 To ensure  regular, adequate, and quality  rural water supply, the Department 
launched "Jal Seva Aankalan " on 30 December 2025. Under this initiative, the  Village 
Water and Sanitation Committee (VWSC) conducts village-level assessments. 
Following a Gram Sabha presentation, the Panchayat Secretary digitally submits the 
data on the  JJM-IMIS Dashboard  for ‘Har Ghar Jal’ villages. District and State 
authorities then review this information to identify service gaps and formulate 
effective improvement plans for sustainable water management. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Third supplementary, Shri P. Wilson.

Framework. But, today, the Union Government says the project stands cancelled and 
suddenly informs that the assistance cannot be extended. Ten additional Multi-village

MR. CHAIRMAN: Now, Q. No.246. 
Target set under National Monetisation Pipeline 2.0 
*246.    SHRI P. WILSON: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to state: 
(a)  the targets set under National Monetisation Pipeline (NMP) 2.0, including 
details of airports identified for monetisation/leasing and the rationale 
therefor; 
(b)  the airport-wise details of development works undertaken in the State of 
Tamil Nadu, including Chennai airport aerobridge capacity, budget 
allocated, expenditure incurred, timelines and reasons for delay, if any; 
and 
(c)  the details of inaugurated airports under the UDAN scheme that have 
suspended operations due to low demand or financial unviability, State-
wise and the details of budget allocated, cumulative losses incurred and 
justification for their establishment? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU):  (a) to 
(c) A statement is laid on the table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) Under NMP 2.0 (FY26-FY30), monetisation target of Rs. 27,500 crore has 
been set for Civil Aviation sector. This includes monetization of 26 airports of Airports 
Authority of India (AAI) namely, Amritsar, Bhubaneswar, Raipur, Trichy, Varanasi, 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
55

 
Kushinagar, Kangra, Gaya, Aurangabad, Hubli, Tirupati, Agartala, Bhopal, Calicut, 
Co
imbatore, Dehradun, Imphal, Indore, Madurai, Patna, Rajahmundry, Ranchi, 
Surat, Udaipur, Vadodara and Vijayawada, expected to contribute Rs. 14,950 crore 
to the monetisation target. Additionally, Rs. 12,550 crore is targeted from divestment 
of AAI's holdings in one of its subsidiaries and four Joint Venture (JV) airports. The 
monetisation proceeds would accrue to AAI for reinvesting into creation of more 
assets and new airport projects. 
 (b) The airport-wise details of development works undertaken in the State of 
Tamil Nadu is enclosed as Annexure.  
 Further, under RCS-UDAN, four airports at Salem, Vellore, Neyveli and 
Thanjavur have been identified for development and operation of flights. An allocation 
of Rs. 134.39 crore has been made, out of which Rs. 115.69 crore has already been 
incurred. Salem Airport was developed and operationalised on 25.03.2018. Physical 
development works at Vellore and Neyveli airports have been completed. Meanwhile, 
works relating to establishment of a civil enclave at Thanjavur airport is being 
undertaken by AAI. 
 The timeline for completion of the airport projects depends upon several 
factors such as land acquisition, availability of mandatory clearances including 
environmental clearances, financial closure etc. 
 (c)  No such airport has suspended operations due to low demand or financial 
unviability. However, operations at some airports have been suspended due to the 
shortage/non-availability of aircraft with airlines, runway works, weather related 
challenges etc. 
 The policy rationale for establishing and reviving these airports is based on the 
objectives of the UDAN scheme, which aim to enhance regional air connectivity and 
make air travel affordable for common citizens, particularly in Tier-II and Tier-III cities 
and remote regions by utilising existing infrastructure such as underutilised airstrips 
and defence or State-owned airfields by upgrading them for civil aviation operations.

suspended shortly after, Chitrakoot inaugurated in March 2024 with Rs. 146 crore 
investment and operations suspended after 8-9 months, Shravasti inaugurated in 
March 2024 with Rs.29 crore investment and operations suspended shortly after, and 
Moradabad inaugurated in March 2024 with operations suspended about three 
months later. These were developed and inaugurated under the UDAN Scheme, but

MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU:  Mr. Chairman, Sir, first of all, regarding the 
question asked by honourable Member, Thambidurai ji, I would like to give a small 
clarification with regard to a couple of things.   Regarding the Parandur Airport, the 
site clearance and in-principle approval has been given. That has also been 
forwarded.  
DR. M. THAMBIDURAI: The land acquisition...  
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: The State Government has to do the land 
acquisition. Land acquisition is now the responsibility of the State Government. With 
regard to Hosur, which is close to Bangalore, as there are a lot of defence-related 
operations in Bangalore, for any kind of additional operations there, the approval has 
to come from Ministry of Defence also. This is what I wanted to bring to the notice of 
honourable Member. Now, coming to the present question regarding the 
monetisation plan, the honourable Member has mentioned the airports, where there 
are no operations happening right now. Mr. Chairman, Sir, with regard to the 
infrastructure projects which are happening, I would like to say that these airports are 
not only to be seen as an immediate connectivity points for today. These are 
infrastructural projects, which are going to be there for the long term. Now, as we go 
ahead, the challenge will be much more when we start building new airports because 
the land availability is going to get much tougher due to rapid urbanization. 
Honourable Chairman, Sir, many of the Members of Parliament are already requesting 
for airports in their vicinity and the first challenge we face, while building a new airport, 
is the land availability. We need something from 700 to almost 1500 acres when we 
want to expand the runway. So, in any case, it is better if we can build an airport 
today for the future, because, definitely, with the improvement in air connectivity and 
the passenger demand that we are seeing, all the airports are going to be operational 
and they are going to be financially viable. That is the point I want to make. Regarding 
these six airports that have been specifically mentioned, it is not a point of low 
demand from that area.  Demand is there from every single area in the country. 
Because of the UDAN (Ude Desh Ka Aam Naagrik) scheme, even in the remote parts 
58
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
of the country, today, the common man is able to purchase a ticket and fly in an 
airc
raft. But, here, the problem is of the availability of aircraft. That is a serious 
bottleneck, which everyone is facing not only in India but also at the international 
level. When COVID happened, the orders went for a toss. The companies, which 
were manufacturing these, suddenly felt that civil aviation is not a very viable sector in 
the future because nobody knew till when the pandemic was going to last or how 
many other pandemic situations may arise in future. So, whoever was placing orders 
for aircraft suddenly stopped that and there was a supply chain disruption. Within that 
aircraft manufacturing also, there is a bottleneck of engine manufacturing also. 
Because of this, the availability of manufactured aircraft has become very, very low. 
Sir, as a country, India also anticipated that with the growth in civil aviation, it will 
have to build more airports and it will also have to bring more aircraft. Accordingly, we 
have built more airports but the coming of aircraft has not taken place as per the 
demand in India. We will soon get more aircraft into our ecosystem. That is why, I 
was concentrating more on having a manufacturing ecosystem of our aircraft here 
also, once we develop our own regional transport aircraft and manufacture it. Many 
players are coming here because of the demand that  is  there in the  country  and  
the Government  is  also  very  positively supporting them so that they can set up their 
manufacturing ecosystems here. Once we do that, then, definitely, all these airports 
where there are no operations today, are going to become operational and financially 
viable also.  
MR. CHAIRMAN: Second Supplementary, Shri P. Wilson. 
SHRI P. WILSON : Sir, several airports are being leased out to the private operators 
who will handle the operations, management and maintenance and who will also fix 
their own charges for the services provided. Why can’t an effective law be brought to 
establish a regulatory mechanism like a commission or a regulatory body to oversee 
and regulate the airfare, advise policy decisions and fix tariff rates and also have an 
appellate authority like the authorities we have in the fields of broadcasting and 
electricity?  Why don't we have a regulatory mechanism for this sector also? 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Honourable Minister. 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU:  Sir, the Air Corporations Act, which was 
regulating the airfares was repealed and the airfares were totally deregulated in the 
90s itself. Internationally also, for the survival of airlines and the viability of operations, 
it was deregulated. It is not one single factor that determines the airfares. There is the 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
59

 
fuel cost; there are multiple other costs that are taken into consideration while 
d
eciding the airfare. So, based on whatever the international practice was, India also 
started following that and deregulation happened. On the other side also, 
deregulation helps in increasing... 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, the House stands adjourned to meet at 2 p.m.

SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu):  Sir, the Appropriation Bill, 2026, authorises the 
Union Government to withdraw an additional two lakh eighty-one thousand two 
hundred eighty-nine crore and twenty-six lakh (Rs. 281,289,26,00,000) rupees from 
the Consolidated Fund of India for the Financial Year 2025-26. Through this 
legislation, the Government is seeking parliamentary approval to appropriate 
massive additional expenditure for 101 items, mentioned in the Schedule to the Bill, 
to various Ministries and Departments. The Schedule to the Bill lists significant 
allocations, including Rs.19,230 crore for the Department of Fertilizers, Rs.23,641 
crore for the Department of Food and Public Distribution, Rs.17,647 crore for the 
Ministry of Road Transport and Highways and several other large sums across 
Departments. My question is : why have more funds to the Food and Public 
Distribution Department not been allotted in view of the LPG scarcity? Another 
problem, which we see, as parliamentarians, whenever the Demands for Grants 
come before us, is that the funds are allocated but the funds are not fully utilised by 
the Departments, and there is no action taken against these Departments which do 
not utilise the funds fully.   
Sir, I see from these additional grant allocations that there is no additional 
demand, so far as the Ministry of Social Justice is concerned. For the Scheduled 
Caste and Scheduled Tribe scholarships, they do not allocate enough funds for the 
marginalised people and these students from the marginalised sections suffer a lot.   
So far as the Department of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare is concerned, 
they have allotted very less amount under the additional grant. But have they 
visualised the problem faced by the farmers? In simple terms, the Bill permits the 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
73

 
Government to dig deeper into the Consolidated Fund of India, the very pool where 
all taxes, revenues, duties, cess collections, borrowings and receipts of the Union 
Government are deposited, and through an Appropriation Bill, the Government 
obtains the authority to withdraw those public resources. Sir, with regard to transfer 
of Rs. 4,04,115 crore to States, let me remind the House of an important fact. The 
Union Government collects 4 per cent as Education Cess from every citizen of India.  
This Cess was introduced specifically to strengthen the education and health sector 
and to support programs, which fulfill the constitutional mandate under Article 21A, 
namely, the Right to Education. The Cess is meant to finance programs like Samagra 
Shiksha Scheme, which supports teachers, salaries, school infrastructure, inclusive 
education for children with disabilities, digital learning, scholarships and universal 
access to quality education. Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, this Cess goes into the 
Consolidated Fund of India, but here is the shocking reality. While the Union 
Government is collecting the Cess from the people of India and putting it into the 
Consolidated Fund of India, it is diverting it through the Appropriation Bill like the one 
which we are discussing here. It is not realizing that the funds are meant for education 
sector in the States like Tamil Nadu. Tamil Nadu alone owes nearly Rs. 3,548 crores 
under this Samagra Shiksha. In its Report of March, 2025, the Parliamentary Standing 
Committee on Education, which included Members from the Ruling Party also, clearly 
stated that the delay in releasing funds under Samagra Shiksha Scheme by the Union 
Government are unjustifiable and it gave directions to immediately release funds. It 
has been one year since the Report has been submitted, but the Union Government is 
not releasing the funds. The Government continues to sit on these funds and is asking 
for another 2.81 lakh crore of rupees to be appropriated from the Consolidated Fund 
of India. Sir, this is fiscal discrimination. The injustice does not stop here. Several 
crucial Central Schemes funds owed to Tamil Nadu remain pending. According to 
detailed representation made by the Tamil Nadu Government, the Union Government 
owes more than 10,000 crore of rupees across various schemes. Sir, take the 
example of Jal Jeevan Mission. The Union Government has released only Rs. 5,914 
crores leaving Rs. 3,100 crores still pending. Take the example of Hogenakkal 
Combined Water Supply. Here, they have dropped the scheme. When we proposed 
to give Rs. 7,590 crores, they said they will give their share, but, today, that is 
dropped! Sir, what message does it give? When it comes to collecting taxes and 
revenues from Tamil Nadu, the Union Government shows no hesitation but when it 
comes to returning funds meant for the welfare schemes, suddenly, the Centre 
develops financial constraints!  
74
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, even under MGNREGA, which supports the poor rural 
hou
seholds, we have Rs. 729.88 crores as pending liabilities for Tamil Nadu, delaying 
wage payments to workers. Under the Finance Commission’s grants for the local 
bodies and disaster preparedness, another Rs. 2,246 crore remain unpaid. In spite of 
all this, the Union Government is asking Parliament to approve another Rs. 2.81 lakh 
crore appropriation from the Consolidated Fund of India. This is nothing but selective 
fiscal governance.   
Sir, I rise to draw the deepening crisis in India's textile sector caused by the 
Government's complete failure to defend our industry against hostile global trade 
developments. India's textile and apparel sector employs more than 45 million 
workers, contributing 2 per cent to the national GDP, 13 per cent to industrial 
production and 12 per cent to India's total exports. Yet, today, this vital sector is 
under severe distress because the Government has failed to protect it from the 
international developments. With the new US-Bangladesh agreement, Bangladeshi 
manufacturers are shifting towards US cotton, putting at risk the Indian cotton and 
yarn exports worth 600 to 650 million US dollars. This is not merely a trade 
adjustment. It is a direct blow to Indian cotton farmers and spinning mills.  The crisis 
has already devastated major textile clusters. In Tamil Nadu, Tirupur is one of India's 
largest knitwear hubs. Exports fell by 37 per cent in 2025, leading to loss of 2 lakh 
jobs. Factories are shutting down; orders are being cancelled and workers are being 
forced to migrate in search of another livelihood. If urgent action is not taken, 
including reducing the tariff imposed by the US, securing tariff parity in global 
markets, removing the cotton import duty and protecting exporters from unfair trade 
practices, India risks losing a sector that sustains millions of livelihoods.                                                                 
3.00 P.M. 
 Sir, now let me talk about the crisis that reflects the Government’s policy 
failure, the LPG situation in the country. The food distribution department is not 
allocated with enough funds. In 2011, when LPG prices were raised by Rs. 50, leaders 
of the present ruling party marched on the streets carrying cylinders and accusing the 
Government of being anti-people and asked where aam admi ki Government is, that 
is, people’s Government. In 2014, when this Government came to power, global 
crude oil prices were roughly in the same range as we see them today. Even today, 
crude oil prices largely hover around 70 dollars to 100 dollars per barrel, which is not 
dramatically different from the global price environment that existed earlier. Yet, 
despite similar crude price levels, the price of the domestic LPG cylinder is almost 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
75

 
double. So, I wanted to ask this Government: Where is a am aadmi ki Government 
that once you promised? When the global crude prices are almost comparable, why 
has the burden on ordinary households increased so drastically? Instead, the burden 
has increased because of the taxation policies and pricing mechanism imposed by the 
Union Government. India consumes about 80,000 tons of LPG per day, roughly 30 to 
31 million tons annually. But our national storage capacity barely covers three to four 
weeks of consumption and strategic reserves are extremely limited. Nearly 60 to 67 
per cent of our LPG is imported mostly from West Asia through the Strait of Hormuz, 
marking India highly vulnerable to geopolitical disruption. Instead of building strategic 
reserves and strengthening supply chains, the Government has allowed supply 
uncertainty and price escalation. Energy experts and policy analysts have been 
warning for years that India's heavy dependence on imported LPG leaves the country 
extremely vulnerable to global disruption. Any geopolitical tension, regional conflict, 
war or blockade in major energy corridors can immediately affect supply chains and 
availability. Today those warnings have become a reality. The consequences are 
severe. In Tamil Nadu alone, over 60,000 factories and commercial establishments 
depend on LPG and similar fuels. Small eateries, hotels and food businesses are 
struggling because of cost surge and erratic supply. While the Union Government has 
failed to act, the Tamil Nadu Government, under the honourable Chief Minister, Mr. 
M.K. Stalin, has stepped forward to protect its people. The State Government has 
introduced electricity subsidies at rupees two per unit for eateries shifting to induction 
cooking. It has provided concessional loans for MSMEs to adopt electric cooking 
equipment. But, Sir, a serious question arises. Across several States, particularly 
those heading to elections, LPG supply appears to be selectively managed, creating 
artificial demand pressures and public dissatisfaction.  If the Government believes that 
political manipulation of essential commodities will help it win elections, it is gravely 
mistaken. The people of Tamil Nadu are politically conscious and socially aware.  
They know who collects the taxes; they know who withholds their funds; they know 
who stands with them in time of crisis.  I would like to quote a Thirukural. and“T he 
world will constantly embrace the feet of the great King who rules over his subjects 
with love. A just king who cherishes his people will have a world that stands by his 
side.” This kural tells us that when a ruler governs by embracing and protecting his 
people, the world itself will stand in the loyalty at its feet. ( Time-bell rings) One 
minute, Sir.  The leader who stands with his people in difficult times earns not just 
authority but the enduring trust of the people.

participate on behalf of AIADMK party and my leader, Edappadi K. Palaniswami and 
support the Appropriation Bill. Sir, the Government led by Prime Minister, Narendra 
Modiji, has shown the country how fiscal responsibility and welfare can coexist. 
Record capital expenditure, infrastructure expansion and targeted welfare programs 
have strengthened India's economic foundation. The Appropriation Bill before this 
House today is yet another demonstration of that commitment of responsible 
governance and national progress.  
Sir, what is even more unfortunate is the repeated attempt to shift the blame 
onto the Central Government by the Tamil Nadu Government. The reality is that Tamil 
Nadu Government has been receiving substantial support through various Central 
schemes and financial transfers. The issue lies not in the availability of funds, but in 
the effective implementation and prioritization. The people of Tamil Nadu deserve a 
governance model that emphasizes sustainable development, efficient utilization of 
resources and cooperative federalism, which DMK Government fails to do. Sir, State 
should work in partnership with the Center to accelerate growth and ensure long-term 
financial-stability.

want to say that the honourable Prime Minister visited the State of Tamil Nadu 
recently, he had made a clear statement that a lot of money is allocated to Tamil

DR. JOHN BRITTAS (Kerala): Sir,  the primary purpose of this is to authorise the 
Government to withdraw or appropriate funds from the Consolidated Fund of India to 
meet the expenditure. Sir, is it not a fact that calculations of the honourable Finance 
Minister have gone for a toss? Is it not a fact that the Budgetary Estimates would go 
awry, would go haywire on account of the headwind that we are facing, the global 
crisis which is there because of the immoral and illegal war that is waged in West 
Asia. I had expected that the Finance Minister, as a preamble, would briefly explain 
about the situation before pursuing with this Bill. Sir, a Covid-like situation has arisen. 
The honourable Minister, Shri Hardeep Singh Puri, is here. He knows that restaurants 
are being closed down everywhere overwhelmingly. In Kerala, colleges are being 
shut. Even hospitals are being closed down because canteens cannot function. Sir, 
my request to my dear friend, honourable Petroleum Minister, is, please take a 
special flight along with his Deputy, Shri Suresh Gopi, let him go to every part of this 
country and see what the position is with regard to the shortage. Please make sure 
that your Deputy, Shri Suresh Gopi, does not depend on filmy dialogues. Please 
restrain him. My submission is that there is a COVID-like situation that is emerging. 
Just banging the plates will not help us resolve this crisis. Listening to the Chief 
Scientist of this nation, hapless people, innocent people are putting their stove on 
drainages and gutter thinking that they will get some gas. That is not the situation. 
Sir, two ships which sailed to our shore, was publicized as though we have broken 
this conundrum. Mr. Hardeep Singh Puri will vouch for the fact that for two days of 
consumption, we need to have three ships to reach our shore.

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA):  The next speaker is Shrimati Sudha 
Murty; ten minutes. 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated):  Sir, normal grants are given during the 
Budget, but you cannot run a nation of 1.4 billion people with a rigid Budget.  It 
requires extra money intermittently and that is the reason our Constitution allows it 
three times in a year. First installment was given in the Winter Session. Now, this is 
due in this Session. SDGF does not run on strict rules. Depending on the ground 
reality, it changes. Being a neutral person, I want to tell this one. Everybody talks that 
gas cylinder price is up. I understand the difficulties of our honourable Petroleum 
Minister. We never started the war. We never asked for rise in gas price. We never 
asked for petrol price rise. It is the whole world which is affected; it is not just India. 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
93

 
So, we should be considerate about why the price has gone up. If the pipelines are 
d
amaged in Iran, we cannot repair it. Please understand it. None of you here, I think, 
in this august gathering, have seen 1962 war. When I was a 10 or 12 years old girl, we 
have undergone what a war means, what sacrifice has to be made in the war and you 
have to adjust!  So, I request everyone to understand the situation. It is not that we 
started this; someone started this and the whole world suffers. 
 In the second Supplementary Demand, it is Rs. 2.81 lakhs crores. Out of that, 
Rs. 0.80 lakhs will be either by recovery or savings from Ministries. So, ultimately, we 
are going to raise almost approximately Rs. 2.8 lakh crore. Running a Budget is like 
running a house. Being a housewife, I know the importance of rainy days, which I 
exercised in my own life, where I saved money without anybody's knowledge and it 
helped and changed my life. Similarly, I congratulate this Government. They have 
started ESF, that is, Economic Stabilisation Fund. That means, for the rainy days, we 
have to save a certain amount of money, which will help. That is Rs. 57,000 crores.  
Unpredictable difficulties have come in our country or, sometimes, even in the world 
for that matter. In 2019-20, it was the COVID era, so we had to undergo difficult time. 
In 2022, it was the Russian-Ukraine war. In 2023, it was Hamas conflict. In 2025, it 
was complex tariff problem. In 2026, it is the West Asia problem. Tell me, when you 
have everyday problem, is it not wise to save some money? So, Rs. 57,000 crores is 
taken from this. We are left with Rs. 1.43 lakh crore in the Supplementary Demand. I 
congratulate the Government starting this very good scheme of ESF. Chanakya or 
Kautilya’s Arthashastra says, ‘What is a prosperous country? A country which has a 
robust defence is a prosperous country’. He gives Saptanga. One of them I am telling 
you. One of them is that you should have an excellent defence system. We have seen 
what has happened in the Operation Sindoor. So, you are giving Rs. 41,000 crores 
approximately to Defence, which is very, very essential. They look after us; our 
soldiers look after us, so that we can live in a democratic India. We should be ever 
grateful to our Armed Force or all three Forces for that matter. So, out of this, money 
is used actually for salary, pension, health and many other issues because they are 
essential. You cannot compromise in that and Rs. 6,100 crores goes to Ex-
Servicemen Contributing Health Scheme.  I have a request to the Government. I may 
suggest a small change for this. We should reconsider change in tax status for 
disability pension for those service people who have attained superannuation because 
they have given a part of their body to our country. So, this is my request to the 
Finance Ministry. In Raghuvansham, Kalidasa describes, through Raja Dilipa, what 
94
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
the meaning of a good nation is and how one should rule nation and what the heart of 
a n
ation is.                                            
4.00 P.M. 
It says that it is the Budget and it is the farmer who makes the difference. Farmer is 
the lifeline of our country and we do not want to keep him or her in the second 
position. He should be given the first priority. So, as regards farmers, we should not 
give them shock due to unpredictable nature of the world. Almost Rs. 9,200 crore has 
been given for the subsidy. Already, we have given, but additional Rs. 9,200 crore is 
given for fertilizers for the farmers. Normally, when you take imported fertilizers, urea 
and other things, 90 per cent of them are imported. And, in today's scenario, it is not 
easy to import these things. So, this additional money is very, very appropriate. This 
is because we should not send a shockwave in our farmer community.   
 The third one is PM Garib Kalyan Yojana. I have to tell two more things about 
the farmers. I am very happy that our Government has taken care for Kharif and Rabi 
both. They have stored enough fertilizers. But, giving this additional money for 
subsidising fertilizer is always important and we should go for that. Then, there is the 
PM Gram Kalyan Yojana for giving the ration. Now, we should, definitely, consider 
this. This is the most important thing because nobody should go hungry with the 
rising prices because of the West Asia’s conflict. We have to take care of the people.   
The prices of everything will go up, but people should not remain hungry. In that 
process, they have given Rs. 23,000 crore. When you deduct all these things, the 
leftover will be Rs. 68,000 crore, which should be used by all the Departments. 
I feel this is a very good Bill and it is very appropriate to give importance to 
Defence, importance to farmers and we are making a new Fund of Rs. 57,000 crore.  
So, I consider this Bill as well designed, appropriation is very nice and I support this 
Bill because a good nation requires a strong defence, a good farmer and a great 
trade, and our country is trying to do all those things. When you do these things, we 
should understand the situation. It is not that somebody is purposely rising the prices.  
The price rise is beyond us. The whole world is suffering. We are part of it. Sir, it is a 
great opportunity that you have given me to talk on this subject. Jai Hind! Vande 
Mataram!

I want to make only three pointed suggestions particularly to the Energy Ministry and
