SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha): “M r. Vice- Chairman, Sir, the Parliament 
authorizes our Government of India to spend money from the Consolidated Fund of 
India for developmental works like education, health, agriculture, and various other 
development projects. This is fine, Sir. However, in our country, 1.5 crore tribal 
people have been displaced and marginalized due to various development projects, 
such as mines, industry, wildlife sanctuaries, national parks, power plants, and 
national highways—especially the tribals of Jharkhand, Odisha, Chhattisgarh, and 
Andhra Pradesh. They are not being properly rehabilitated or resettled. Funds should 
be allotted for that. In the Sardar Sarovar Dam, 85,000 tribals were displaced; in the 
Polavaram Dam project, 300 tribal villages are being displaced. They should be 
resettled suitably. Sir, in our State, Odisha, 5,632 government schools have been 
closed in tribal villages and rural areas; funds should be allotted for the reopening of 
those schools. In our Anwesha Hostels, where ST/SC students used to study, 
admissions have been closed for the last two years. The Government of India should 
allot funds so that ST/SC students can study in private English medium schools. 
Also, for the ST/SC students who were pursuing GNM and BSc Nursing course, they 
used to receive ₹50,000 per year for their studies, but that has also been stopped. 
The Government should provide stipends and allot funds for these ST/SC children. 
Furthermore, in the NSFDC (National SC/ST Finance and Development 
Corporation), no tribals or SC/ST individuals are receiving any kind of loan, subsidy, 
or concession; funds should be allotted for this. Also, the Government should allot 
funds for the livelihood of our PVTGs (Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups). Under 
TRIFED, the minor forest produces that tribals collect and sell to earn a living are not 
getting any MSP (Minimum Support Price), no input subsidies, and there are no 
market facilities. Therefore, the lives of the 15 crore tribals in India have become 
miserable.  It was Baba Saheb Ambedkar's dream to bring the ST/SC people, who 
are at the last rung of India's ladder and are neglected, into the mainstream of the 
country. These ST/SCs are a neglected, exploited, oppressed, and toiling mass; the 
"Voice of the Voiceless" for these tribal and Dalit brothers must be heard. Whether it 
is the Consolidated Fund, the TSP (Tribal Sub-Plan), or the SCSP (Scheduled Caste 
 
 E nglish translation of the original speech delivered in Odia.  
102
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
Sub-Plan), funds should be provided from these to include ST/SCs in the national 
main
stream then only the dream of our Baba Saheb Ambedkar will be fulfilled.”                           
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA): The next speaker is Shri S Niranjan 
Reddy; twelve minutes.  
SHRI S NIRANJAN REDDY (Andhra Pradesh): Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, while this is a 
recurring feature in relation to the Supplementary Demands for Grants, there are two 
issues that keep coming every year. I want to focus on these two issues which are 
food and fertilizer. The third issue I want to speak about today would be on transfers 
to States. On the first issue of food, the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and 
Public Distribution seeks an additional Rs. 23,641.28 crores under the Pradhan Mantri 
Garib Kalyan Anna Yojana. This is important. This needs to be sanctioned. But, I have 
a few concerns. It is not just about this Supplementary Demand in relation to this 
amount for this year. As of January 2026, as we speak, the foodgrain stocks in India 
are 59.45 million tonnes. The required buffer norm for India's population would be 
21.41 million tonnes. So, we are having much more than the required buffer stocks. I 
would want to elaborate a little to explain how flawed agricultural policies are leading 
to this lopsided development of certain cropping patterns and mono-cropping 
patterns in India. When we carry foodgrain stocks of 59.45 million tonnes, and when 
India is required to hold 21.41 million tonnes, undisputably, this means that we have 
to carry additional, substantial carrying and storage cost. We need godowns and we 
need to ensure that foodgrains are stored properly so that they do not spoil. So, this 
additional carrying and storage cost is all money that is going waste.  It is because, 
we have the Minimum Support Prices to farmers—which is recommendatory—and I 
find, since this Minimum Support Prices are being granted without a lot of 
forethought, this is leading to changing crop patterns and increasing monocropping, 
as I mentioned. So, this is resulting in India producing more wheat and more rice, 
including in States that traditionally have not been rice-growing States. This is 
resulting in India stocking up these buffers while India has been slowly reducing the 
growth of pulses, edible oils and other things.                
 So there is an increasing import bill. On the one hand, we have an increasing 
cost in relation to procuring food grains that we do not need, while on the other hand, 
India is spending more money on importing food and oil items that are needed for 
India. This needs to be attended to by the Government in the longer run; otherwise, 
every year, we will have the supplementary demand for grants in relation to food. 
Usually, the Comptroller and Auditor General applies what is known as the cost-
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
103

 
benefit ratio; if we are spending one rupee from the Indian Government, what is the 
b
enefit that we get? Sir, look at the two crops that I specifically mentioned. There 
may be other crops. I am not too much of an agriculturalist. I am not an expert, there 
are other people who know this more, but I am just focusing onto for explaining the 
symptoms of the problem.  In relation to rice and wheat, we have to factor in first, the 
fertilizer cost and I will be dealing about that in a few minutes. We have to factor in the 
MSP cost, which is higher than the cost that is generally available in the market. 
Third, we have to factor in the energy cost because most State Governments are 
giving free power by subsidizing it, which is not coming in the cost. Fourth, we have 
to factor in environmental cost. We have seen that in States like Punjab, now 
increasingly in Telangana, because people have shifted to growth of rice in huge 
quantities, with huge fertilizers that are being used, it is resulting in water depletion, it 
is resulting in percolation of fertilizers in the ground.  
[THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK) in the Chair.] 
This is going to have a long-term effect on the country's health and our 
environment. In the cost benefit ratio, one way is to factor in the environmental cost.  
The fifth item is the water cost. Madam, I attended a lecture by one agricultural 
scientist, who is a very celebrated member of NITI Aayog formerly. He mentioned that 
for every kg of rice that is produced, we consume 200 kgs. of water. Even if India 
imports one kg of rice--it may be more than 200 kgs, I am being conservative; I do 
not want to err--if India is stocking 30 million metric tons of rice, we need to 
recognize that on each of these kgs. of rice, India has wasted 200 kgs. of fresh water. 
Fresh water is an area that is going to be a great concern in future. If we add the 
storage and carrying cost to this, we will have the cost on one side of producing one 
kg of rice or one kg of wheat which we are storing in our godowns because nobody 
requires it at this particular point of time. It is in excess of the buffer-stock we need. 
What do we do? Madam, I ask myself. What we now do is, we understand that this 
rice is not needed for consumption, this wheat is not needed for consumption, we are 
giving it at a great subsidy for ethanol production. If the procurement price of the 
Central Government is one rupee, at a discount, it is selling this rice for ethanol 
production. Ethanol production is salutary. It helps us improve our self-reliability in 
relation to petroleum and gas products. But we may have to follow what Brazil is 
doing. We may have to grow crops that will help us increase our ethanol rather than 
over-produce two or three items for which we then pay more, suffer environmental 
consequences and because we have no other option, give it to ethanol production.  
104
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
The second item I wanted to speak was on Ministry of Chemicals and 
Fer
tilizers because in the supplementary demands, it requires Rs.15,000 crore 
additional allocation under the nutrient-based subsidy. Madam, you will be surprised 
that while India is highly dependent and, this is a known fact, importing 100 per cent 
of potash requirements and 90 per cent of phosphatic raw materials, the 
recommended nitrogen, phosphorous, potassium ratio for Indian soil is 
approximately, 4:2:1. I repeat, Madam, it is 4:2:1. But, in agriculturally intensive States 
such as Punjab and Haryana, the ratio of fertilizer being applied by farmers -- I remind 
everyone the requirement is 4 :2:1, -- comes to 30 :10:1!  We are providing more than 
seven times the required ratio or five times the required ratio because farmers have 
not been properly trained and also because we have not incentivized the farmers. The 
fertilizer subsidy is being given to fertilizer manufacturers.  We are not applying a 
cost-benefit ratio and giving this fertilizer benefit directly to farmers, which is resulting 
in farmers getting fertilizers and using them indiscriminately. This is leading to burden 
our costs and our Budget. Every year, we spend money on fertilizers through 
Supplementary Demands. If we are able to have a five-year or ten-year plan, over a 
period of time, we reduce this cost on food and fertilizer. At this point, it makes good 
politics because nobody wants to say anything against farmers’ interests. I am not 
speaking against farmers’ interests. I am saying, ‘please protect the farmers.’  But, 
while protecting them, if we can save these costs, the resources can instead be used 
by India for education, health, and defence.   
Madam, the third issue on which I want to speak about is transfers to States.  
In the Supplementary Demands for Grants, I noticed certain amount is sought for 
transfers to States in relation to panchayats and urban local bodies. I recognize that I 
am speaking in the Council of States. But, it has been a long time since I have heard a 
meaningful debate. And, I am being very honest about this Council speaking about 
States’ interests.  Everybody is parroting the party lines. There is absolutely no clarity 
between the Union’s interest and the States’ interests. Both are in national interest. I 
am happy that the Central Government is making allocations to the State 
Governments, for urban local bodies and panchayats, because the Central 
Government recognizes that the States are not making adequate provisions for them.  
Very few States have a planning for urban cities. Forty per cent of India is going to be 
living in urban cities; yet, many States are not bothered about having a plan. They are 
only concerned about five-year elections. They work to win the election, get past this 
election and say, we will see in the next election. There needs to be a forward-looking 
plan that needs to be done. I would like to remind the Central Government that this 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
105

 
cannot be a long-term plan. I understand, they are doing this because States are not 
d
oing their duty. Please incentivize States in a different mechanism when you are 
providing for the sharing of the tax resources to States that are making these 
provisions, rather than you give it out. And, I must again say this candidly that you 
give it out to the States where you are in power in the State. This is not the right way 
of giving it out. The statistics show that this is being done in a partisan manner. The 
best way is to please encourage the States to do it and you make allocations.  
Madam, I recognize that maybe the BJP-ruled States are fiscally the best and most 
responsible States. I am not from that party. But, while I say that, I am saying: Please 
incentivize every party, to have a fiscal responsibility by giving more rather than 
looking at electoral benefits and trying to allocate resources. Madam, I have a few 
more points to make. But, I want to be mindful and stay within my time. I am thankful 
for the opportunity granted to me.                                                                          
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK): Shri Sandosh 
Kumar P. 
SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P (Kerala):  Madam Vice-Chairman, today, uncertainty 
looms large over the Indian economy due to external shocks, many of which have 
been aggravated by the policies of the BJP Government. The ongoing conflict in the 
West Asia is not a distant political issue for India; it has direct consequences for our 
trade, employment, remittances and energy security. Unfortunately, despite the 
gravity of the situation, the Union Government appears unprepared. Let us 
understand the scale of India’s economic dependence on the Gulf region. India’s total 
trade with the Gulf Cooperation Council countries is around 178 billion US dollars, and 
our exports to these countries are roughly 56–57 billion US dollars annually .                                                          
These exports include agricultural products, food items, textiles, engineering goods 
and pharmaceuticals. These sectors employ millions of workers across rural and 
semi-urban India. If the conflict continues and trade routes are disrupted, these 
exports will decline. This will directly affect farmers, small producers and workers, 
leading to unemployment, particularly in the rural economy. During such times, the 
rural economy earlier had a cushion in the form of MGNREGA, a right-based program 
that provided livelihood security. But, this Government has systematically weakened 
it. The so-called VB-G RAM G framework is still not even notified. And, the 
allocations are already too low to meaningfully deliver the promised 125 days of 
employment. When export opportunities sink and agriculture incomes fall, how 
exactly does the Government intend to protect rural livelihoods?  
106
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
The second concern relates to remittances. Madam, nearly 9 to 10 million 
Indians work in the Gulf region and India receives over 129 billion U . S. dollars annually 
in remittances--the highest in the world. A significant portion, I mean, roughly 1/3rd to 
nearly 40 per cent, comes from Gulf countries. These remittances sustain millions of 
families and support the economies of several States. If the conflict persists and 
economic activity slows in the Gulf, employment opportunities for Indian workers may 
shrink and remittances may decline. This will have serious consequences for 
households, regional economies and our foreign exchange flows. What contingency 
plans does the Government have to deal with such a situation? How will it support 
returning workers and protect the families that depend on these incomes? Another 
concern is energy security. 
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK): Please conclude.  
SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P : India imports more than 85 per cent of its crude oil 
requirements; much of it is linked to supplies from West Asia. So, the Government 
should take necessary steps to deal with the situation. W84 ,I¥HC ; G  H.sz ; G  <3T

THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK): Further discussion 
on the Appropriation Bill, 2026 will continue on Tuesday, the 17 th March, 2026. Now, 
we 
will take up Special Mentions. Shriman Brij Lal.  
 
SPECIAL MENTIONS 
Need for nature-based solutions and risk-informed planning to Urban Planners 
SHRI BRIJ LAL (Uttar Pradesh): Madam, my subject is- Need for Nature-Based 
Solutions and Risk-Informed Planning to Urban Planners. In a recent Case Studies 
Compendium released by the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA), in 
February 2026, the agency identified a clear link between poor urban planning, land-
use changes, and the severity of recent disasters. Ecological Lessons for Urban 
Planners. The NDMA emphasizes a "paradigm shift" from traditional engineering to 
nature-based solutions and risk-informed planning: (i) Mainstreaming Climate Risk: 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
121

 
Planners must incorporate geological, seismic, and climate risk evaluations into 
te
chnical feasibility studies, rather than relying solely on traditional engineering 
parameters. (ii) Protecting Natural Infrastructure: Cities are advised to restore urban 
wetlands, protect floodplains, and create green buffers to manage runoff naturally. 
(iii) Permeable Surfaces: Using permeable and rainwater harvesting is highlighted as 
a cost-effective way to reduce urban flood peaks, which can be 8 times higher in 
developed catchments. (iv) Encroachment Removal: A critical lesson is the urgent 
need to remove encroachments from natural water channels and drains to prevent 
catastrophic urban flooding. If all the above lessons attained from the last 10 recent 
disasters occurred from 2021 to 2024 recommended by NDMA are implemented in 
letter and spirit, we will be able to prevent such disasters in future. I urge the 
Government to look into it. Thank you, Madam.  
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK): The following 
honourable Members associated themselves with the Special Mention made by 
honourable Member, Shri Brij Lal:  Dr. Fauzia Khan (Maharashtra), Shri Kesridevsinh 
Jhala (Gujarat), Shrimati Ramilaben Becharbhai Bara (Gujarat) and Shri Banshilal 
Gurjar (Madhya Pradesh). 
 Shriman Dr. Ajeet Madhavrao Gopchade.

Need for aggressive outreach to register Unorganised Workers on  
the e-Shram Portal 
SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P (Kerala): Madam, I seek to draw attention of the 
Government to the urgent need to strengthen outreach efforts for the registration of 
workers on the e-Shram portal, which was launched to create a comprehensive 
database of workers in the unorganised sector and extend social security benefits to 
them. India has nearly 44 crore workers in the unorganised sector, including 
construction workers, domestic workers, street vendors, agricultural labourers, gig 
workers and migrant workers. These workers form the backbone of our economy but 
remain highly vulnerable due to the absence of adequate social security protection. 
The e-Shram portal is an important initiative aimed at registering such workers and 
linking them to welfare measures such as accident insurance, pension schemes and 
other social security benefits. However, despite the progress made so far, a large 
number of workers are not registered on the portal. One of the major reasons is the 
lack of awareness among workers, especially, in rural areas and among migrant 
labourers. Many workers are not aware of the benefits of e-Shram registration or the 
simple procedures involved in enrolling themselves on the portal. As a result, millions 
of eligible workers continue to remain outside the ambit of social security. There is, 
therefore, an urgent need for the Government to conduct aggressive awareness 
campaigns and outreach programmes across the country. Registration camps, 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
123

 
community-level awareness drives, and the involvement of local bodies, trade unions 
a
nd civil society organisations can help ensure that every eligible worker is brought 
under the e-Shram database. I urge the Government to look into it.

Concern over the healthcare services in Kerala due to series of ongoing strikes 
SHRIMATI JEBI MATHER HISHAM (Kerala): Madam, Kerala, a State long celebrated 
for its exemplary healthcare system, is witnessing an unprecedented disruption in 
medical services. With nurses on strike in private hospitals and doctors protesting in 
Government hospitals, the healthcare system is facing a severe standstill. Several 
private hospitals are affected, with thousands of nurses participating in protests 
demanding fair wages and humane working conditions. Surgeries are being 
postponed, admissions restricted, and patients are forced to move from hospital to 
hospital in search of care. At the center of this crisis lies a shocking reality. The basic 
salary of many nurses in private hospitals has remained Rs. 20,000 per month since 
2018. For eight long years, despite inflation, rising costs of living, and the 
extraordinary sacrifices made during the COVID-19 pandemic, these frontline 
healthcare workers have seen virtually no meaningful revision in their wages. If 
Kerala’s hospitals begin to fall silent because its nurses are unheard, the responsibility 
will not lie with the nurses but with the system that ignored them. I urge upon the 
Government to intervene and ensure a fair national framework for nurses’ wages and 
their working conditions.                                                                     
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK ): The following

honourable Member, Shrimati Jebi Mather Hisham: Shri Haris Beeran (Kerala), and 
Shri Ashok Singh (Madhya Pradesh).

Concern over adverse impact of India-US trade deal on agricultural interests 
D
R. SYED NASEER HUSSAIN (Karnataka): Madam, there is anxiety in India's 
farming community over the agricultural concessions quietly embedded in the India-
US interim trade deal. Under the deal, India has agreed to eliminate or reduce tariffs 
on all US industrial goods and a wide range of US food and agricultural products, 
including tree nuts, fresh and processed fruit, soybean oil, wine and spirits. India has 
also committed to purchasing 500 billion US dollars of US energy, aircraft, 
technology, and coking coal over the next five years -- an astronomical sum that 
crowds out domestic investment priorities and constrains future budget flexibility. 
Additionally, the situation in the West Asia threatens to further affect the plight of 
farmers, as India imports nearly 40 per cent of its Urea and NPK fertilizers from the 
West Asia, critical for the upcoming Kharif season. Moreover, recently, over 400,000 
tons of Basmati rice were reported stuck at ports due to disruption of trade routes to 
Saudi Arabia and Iran. I urge the Government to look into it. 
THE VICE- CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK): The following 
honourable Members associated themselves with the Special Mention made by the 
honourable Member, Dr. Syed Naseer Hussain: Dr. V. Sivadasan (Kerala), Dr. Fauzia 
Khan (Maharashtra), Shri Haris Beeran (Kerala), Shri Ashok Singh (Madhya 
Pradesh), Shrimati Jebi Mather Hisham (Kerala) Shrimati Mahua Maji (Jharkhand) 
and Dr. John Brittas (Kerala) 
 Now, Shri Mahendra Bhatt.

Concern over town planning recruitment and regulation 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra):  Madam, there seems to be little concern when 
our cities are planned by those without formal training in the discipline. The effects are 
visible--congested roads, shrinking green spaces, and cities expanding without 
adequate regard for topography or demography. Town planning is a specialized, 
legally recognized discipline, regulated by the UGC and AICTE, and distinct from 
architecture and engineering. Nearly 100 institutions produce around 2,000 qualified 
planners annually. Yet, many State Governments continue recruiting persons without 
planning qualifications to permanent posts, partly because no statutory framework 
exists to guide otherwise. There is also a structural gap worth addressing. Town 
planning is listed under the Twelfth Schedule as a function of Urban Local Bodies; yet 
in practice, it remains with State Governments, whose departments exercise 
significant powers of sanctioning building plans, regulating land use, and preparing 
master plans. The numbers deserve attention. Analysis indicates that in Karnataka, 
only 30 percent of officers are trained planners; in Telangana, 28 per cent; in Kerala, 
32 per cent. The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs issued advisories in 2017, 
2019, and 2022, and the NITI Aayog made similar recommendations in 2021. The 
implications are real.  
Public investment in planning education is under-utilized. Cities bearing the 
cost of suboptimal decisions, and qualified professionals unable to contribute where 
they are most needed. A statutory Council for Town and Country Planners, 
[ 16 March, 2026 ]
127

 
established by an Act of Parliament, would be a reasonable and necessary step 
f
orward.  I urge the Government to look into it.                                                                                     
THE VICE- CHAIRPERSON (SHRIMATI S . PHANGNON KONYAK):  The following 
honourable Members associated themselves with the matter raised by the honourable 
Member, Dr . Fauzia Khan : Dr . V . Sivadasan (Kerala), Shrimati Mahua Maji 
(Jharkhand), Shrimati Jebi Mather Hisham (Kerala) and Dr. John Brittas (Kerala).                                                  
 Now, Shri Shambhu Sharan Patel.
